                                        <path fill-rule="evenodd" clip-rule="evenodd" fill="#FFFFFF" d="M45.201,12.343c0.378,0.48,0.758,0.925,1.096,1.401    c2.975,4.207,4.543,8.876,4.494,14.044c-0.05,5.452-1.643,10.386-5.186,14.593c-3.484,4.133-7.929,6.73-13.182,7.895    c-6.313,1.398-12.216,0.275-17.695-3.131c-0.441-0.273-0.847-0.6-1.266-0.904c-0.11-0.078-0.208-0.174-0.337-0.287    c0.127-0.141,0.246-0.27,0.366-0.398c0.887-0.949,1.765-1.904,2.663-2.844c0.114-0.119,0.321-0.217,0.485-0.217    c3.658-0.006,7.318,0,10.975,0.008c3.458,0.006,6.913,0.02,10.369,0.02c0.957,0,1.871-0.193,2.62-0.844    c0.797-0.693,1.157-1.596,1.157-2.643c0.001-7.533,0.003-15.067-0.005-22.601c-0.002-0.309,0.088-0.524,0.3-0.743    C43.098,14.598,44.127,13.49,45.201,12.343"></path>
                                        <path fill-rule="evenodd" clip-rule="evenodd" fill="#FFFFFF" d="M41.402,8.822c-0.99,1.027-1.994,2.021-2.935,3.072    c-0.312,0.35-0.616,0.416-1.036,0.415c-6.98-0.009-13.957-0.007-20.938-0.007c-2.039,0-3.561,1.514-3.561,3.557    c0,6.504,0.002,13.008,0.006,19.512c0.002,0.973,0.011,1.943,0.004,2.914c0,0.133-0.04,0.301-0.127,0.393    c-1.069,1.162-2.15,2.314-3.229,3.469c-0.021,0.023-0.052,0.039-0.109,0.08c-0.159-0.188-0.323-0.369-0.471-0.562    c-2.535-3.348-4.119-7.102-4.605-11.268c-0.61-5.229,0.194-10.229,2.835-14.839c2.669-4.664,6.655-7.805,11.618-9.75    c3.205-1.257,6.533-1.852,9.977-1.621c4.478,0.298,8.553,1.754,12.227,4.325c0.101,0.072,0.197,0.151,0.291,0.229    C41.364,8.755,41.374,8.778,41.402,8.822"></path>
                                        <path fill-rule="evenodd" clip-rule="evenodd" fill="#FFFFFF" d="M39.799,12.47c0.873-0.911,1.749-1.829,2.676-2.797    c0.605,0.564,1.195,1.112,1.816,1.691c-0.941,0.985-1.817,1.903-2.703,2.83c-0.276-0.339-0.511-0.688-0.807-0.975    C40.492,12.941,40.145,12.728,39.799,12.47"></path>
                                        <path fill-rule="evenodd" clip-rule="evenodd" fill="#FFFFFF" d="M10.35,43.279c0.969-1.016,1.885-1.977,2.76-2.893    c0.213,0.369,0.376,0.762,0.639,1.072c0.265,0.312,0.627,0.539,0.98,0.832c-0.853,0.891-1.713,1.791-2.624,2.746    C11.513,44.445,10.939,43.869,10.35,43.279"></path>

<article class="post tag-ctir-trends tag-cisco-talos-incident-response tag-ctir-trends-2 tag-hash-tir-blade-posts featured  blog-series-article">

    <h1 class="text-center">IR trends Q3 2026: Actors impersonate IT help desks in vishing attacks as ransomware activity increases</h1>

    <div class="text-center m-1">
        <div class="post-author">
            <span>By </span>
                <a href="https://blog.talosintelligence.com/author/aliza/">Aliza Johnson</a>
        </div>

        <br/>
        <time class="post-datetime" datetime="October 29, 2026 10:00">
            Thursday, October 29, 2026 10:00
        </time>

        <div class="m-3">
                        <a href="https://blog.talosintelligence.com/category/ctir-trends/" class="category primary-category">
                            Talos IR trends
                        </a>
                                            <a href="https://blog.talosintelligence.com/category/cisco-talos-incident-response/" class="category primary-category">
                            Cisco Talos Incident Response
                        </a>
                                            <a href="https://blog.talosintelligence.com/category/ctir-trends-2/" class="category primary-category">
                            CTIR trends
                        </a>
                            </div>
    </div>

    <section class="post-content-wrapper mt-5">
        <div class="post-content" id="post-content">
            <p>Phishing remained the leading initial access technique, appearing in slightly over one-third of all Cisco Talos Incident Response (Talos IR) engagements —&nbsp;a decrease from over 50 percent last quarter. In addition to traditional phishing links and attachments, we observed numerous threat actors conducting voice phishing (vishing) in tandem with IT desk impersonation to socially engineer the victim into allowing remote access via a legitimate remote monitoring and management (RMM) tool. Exploitation of public-facing applications was the next most common vector, accounting for one-third of Talos IR engagements. Threat actors predominantly targeted edge devices such as security gateways and remote network management interfaces. These are critical components within enterprise environments, often responsible for managing authentication, network traffic, or sensitive data. Compromising them can provide broad access to internal networks or user credentials.&nbsp;</p><p>Ransomware and pre-ransomware incidents made up 27 percent of engagements in Q3 2026, an increase from a maximum of 20 percent seen in the previous four quarters. Talos responded to attacks involving prolific, technologically advanced, and well-established ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) variants such as The Gentlemen, Qilin, and Rhysida, as well as Interlock, which operates as a closed group. Threat actors predominantly chose to disable endpoint detection and response (EDR) systems, often by exploiting vulnerable drivers or using Windows Defender Application Control (WDAC). Additionally, we observed the emerging ransomware variant VYPR, whose operator(s) tested a range of tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs) instead of following a standard attack method or affiliate playbook.</p><h2 id="salt-typhoon-affiliated-threat-actors-exploit-public-facing-application-vulnerabilities-for-cyber-espionage">Salt Typhoon-affiliated threat actors exploit public-facing application vulnerabilities for cyber espionage&nbsp;</h2><p>Talos IR continues to see prolonged cyber espionage intrusions targeting the telecommunications sector. Talos attributed most of these intrusions with high confidence to China state-sponsored threat actors, including Salt Typhoon. The attackers in these intrusions routinely gain initial access by leveraging valid credentials and exploiting a known vulnerability (CVE-2023-20198) in publicly accessible web-based management interfaces—two of the most frequently observed initial access vectors this quarter. In one campaign, the threat actor deployed <a href="https://blog.talosintelligence.com/active-exploitation-of-cisco-ios-xe-software/" rel="noreferrer noopener"><u>BadCandy,</u></a> a malicious web shell implant known to be leveraged by Salt Typhoon, to maintain persistent and stealthy remote access.&nbsp;</p><p>The adversaries continue to employ living-off-the-land (LOTL) tactics, leveraging legitimate network infrastructure features — such as VPN access, traffic mirroring, and management protocols — to establish backdoors, exfiltrate data, and conduct reconnaissance. Exploiting a vulnerability in the web-based management interface, they created privileged accounts, disabled logging to conceal their activity, and altered configurations to maintain persistence, blending their actions with normal administrative operations to evade detection.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p>These intrusions revealed several security weaknesses in the victim environment, including reliance on password-only authentication without evidence of multi-factor authentication (MFA), use of legacy and weak management protocols (such as plaintext SNMP and outdated VPN encryption), and inadequate credential management practices. Attackers were able to create and abuse privileged accounts, disable logging, and manipulate configuration settings without encountering effective security barriers, enabling persistent and stealthy access. The absence of strong MFA and robust credential management are two of the most frequently observed security weaknesses this quarter.&nbsp;</p><h2 id="ai-exploited-as-a-weapon-and-a-target">AI exploited as a weapon and a target&nbsp;</h2><p>Recent Talos IR investigations highlight the growing role of artificial intelligence (AI) in cybercriminal activity, both as a tool and a target. In one case, an internal threat actor who was hired under false credentials abused their privileged access to install AI-powered development tools, such as GitHub Copilot CLI and Anthropic Claude Code. While the investigation did not find evidence of malicious activity or data theft, the employee’s developer-level access — including cloning repositories, configuring plugins, and connecting to databases — could have enabled them to use AI tools to automate malicious actions within the organization.&nbsp;</p><p>A second investigation revealed an attack chain in which a compromised macOS endpoint, infected via a trojanized application, was scripted to scrape credentials from the macOS keychain specifically associated with the Anthropic Claude Code AI assistant. This direct targeting of AI-related secrets highlights an emerging threat, where attackers seek to compromise not only traditional user and system credentials but also those tied to AI platforms, which may grant access to additional sensitive data and advanced automation capabilities.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p>These incidents highlight the critical need for organizations to implement strong MFA and robust credential management practices, especially for AI platforms and development tools.&nbsp;</p><h2 id="ransomware-trends">Ransomware trends&nbsp;</h2><p>Ransomware and pre-ransomware incidents increased from a little over 20 percent last quarter to 27 percent in Q3, involving well-known threat groups like Interlock and the emerging ransomware VYPR. Frequently observed techniques involved deleting EDR systems and socially engineering victims into initiating remote management sessions.</p><h3 id="emerging-ransomware-variant-vypr">Emerging ransomware variant: VYPR&nbsp;</h3><p>During a Talos IR engagement, we observed a ransomware variant called “VYPR” that we assess is relatively new and still operationally immature. This assessment is supported by the absence of an established data leak site (DLS), as none was referenced in the ransom note and associated DLS infrastructure was not created until after the engagement. The group also appeared to rely on older leaked Conti-derived source code with only minimal modifications, reflecting a relatively unsophisticated technical approach. Their operations further suggested experimentation rather than a refined playbook, as they took an unusually long time to exfiltrate and encrypt data while testing different exfiltration parameters and deploying multiple vulnerable drivers to disable EDR protections.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p>The actor gained initial access through voice phishing (vishing); they tricked a user into initiating a Microsoft Quick Assist session, allowing remote access to the endpoint. While the exact social engineering technique was not confirmed in this case, threat actors frequently impersonate IT help desks during vishing calls to manipulate victims into establishing remote connections — a tactic observed in several other Q3 engagements.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p>The actor was then able to utilize existing RMM software, which subsequently executed PowerShell commands to download and install a Microsoft Software Installer (MSI) package from a remote IP address. During the attack, the actor leveraged tools that are commonly observed during ransomware investigations, including MeshAgent, Rclone, PsExec, IP scan, and Microsoft Terminal Services Client (MSTSC). Notably, we saw exfiltration over multiple days, which is not commonly observed during ransomware investigations.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><h3 id="edr-killers-why-evade-detection-when-you-can-destroy-it">EDR killers: Why evade detection when you can destroy it?&nbsp;&nbsp;</h3><p>We observed several instances where ransomware operators disabled EDR systems, prioritizing direct disruption of security controls. We observed Interlock ransomware actors making multiple attempts to impair endpoint detection EDR capabilities by trying to uninstall Cisco Secure Endpoint (CSE) on compromised systems and used tools such as PsExec.exe and taskkill.exe to terminate security services and integrity checkers, including cscm.exe and sfc.exe. Additionally, the adversary disabled Windows Defender real-time protection and cleared various event logs to reduce visibility and hinder forensic analysis. This is in line with previously observed Interlock activity, where they deployed a malicious WDAC policy to terminate EDR solutions.&nbsp;</p><p>In the previously mentioned VYPR ransomware incident, the threat actor disabled EDR and antivirus (AV) by using Bring Your Own Vulnerable Driver (BYOVD) techniques to evade detection and execute the ransomware. BYOVD attacks take advantage of trusted but flawed drivers to gain kernel-level access, enabling threat actors to disable EDR mechanisms and carry out their operations unimpeded. While this technique is commonly observed in ransomware attacks, this actor deployed an unusually large number of vulnerable drivers in the environment, possibly to create redundancies and maximize their chances of success.&nbsp;</p><h2 id="targeting">Targeting&nbsp;</h2><p>Targeting of the financial industry increased from last quarter to comprise 18 percent of all engagements, tying with public administration as the most targeted sector. Although the financial industry remains a valuable target, Talos has not ranked it among the top three most impacted sectors since 2024. One possible reason is that organizations in finance can typically invest more in cybersecurity, unlike sectors such as public administration, which has consistently been among the top-targeted industries over the past year. Meanwhile, health care fell to third place after ranking as the most targeted industry for the previous two consecutive quarters.</p><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3Targets-dark.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="2000" height="876" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3Targets-dark.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3Targets-dark.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3Targets-dark.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w2400/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3Targets-dark.jpg 2400w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><h2 id="initial-access">Initial access&nbsp;</h2><p>While the number of engagements involving phishing as an initial access vector declined from last quarter, it remained the most common technique, appearing in 37 percent of Talos IR engagements. Exploitation of public-facing applications was the second most common initial access vector, accounting for 33 percent of engagements.</p><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-06.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="2000" height="758" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-06.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-06.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-06.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w2400/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-06.jpg 2400w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><h2 id="recommendations-for-addressing-top-security-weaknesses">Recommendations for addressing top security weaknesses&nbsp;</h2><p>The most common security weaknesses observed this quarter were insufficient logging, improper credential and secret management, and absent or improperly implemented authentication and MFA. These trends closely mirror last quarter, when insufficient logging and authentication weaknesses were also prevalent.</p><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-02.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="2000" height="758" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-02.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-02.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-02.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w2400/2026/10/Q3dark_Q3SecurityWeakness-dark-02.jpg 2400w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><h3 id="centralize-logging-and-set-retention-requirements">Centralize logging and set retention requirements&nbsp;</h3><p>The leading security weakness was insufficient logging leading to visibility gaps, seen in over 40 percent of engagements this quarter. These gaps were in part due to the common threat actor practice of deleting logs, and in part due to insufficient log retention or configuration implemented by the customer. Talos IR recommends implementing a SIEM or centralized logging platform with a minimum of 90 days of retention, forwarding logs from servers, workstations, network infrastructure, cloud identity providers, and security appliances off-device so they survive log tampering and host rebuilds, and enabling process-creation, command-line, and cloud API (e.g., Microsoft Graph) auditing. Talos IR's <a href="https://talosintelligence.com/incident_response/assessment" rel="noreferrer noopener"><u>Log Architecture Assessment</u></a> service can identify gaps and provide a roadmap to a complete view of the environment.&nbsp;</p><h3 id="tighten-authentication-and-mfa-controls">Tighten authentication and MFA controls&nbsp;</h3><p>Missing or insufficient authentication and MFA controls were the third most common security weakness this quarter, observed in 33 percent of engagements. The most prevalent issue was reliance on single-factor authentication, which allowed unauthorized access in several incidents. In many cases, legacy authentication protocols were still enabled, enabling adversaries to log in without any MFA requirements, and some environments failed to enforce MFA for high-privilege accounts. To reduce this risk, Talos IR recommends transitioning from push- and SMS-based MFA to phishing-resistant methods such as FIDO2/WebAuthn and hardware security keys. Organizations should also restrict self-service MFA enrollment by requiring helpdesk verification, block legacy authentication through Conditional Access, enforce number matching or verified push where phishing-resistant methods are not yet feasible, and condition access on device compliance and trusted infrastructure rather than geographic location alone.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><h3 id="safeguard-credentials-and-secrets">Safeguard credentials and secrets&nbsp;</h3><p>Improper credential and secret management was the second most common security weakness seen in 33 percent of engagements this quarter, reinforcing the importance of security awareness training and consistent security hygiene for every user. Observed security issues included unsafe storage and handling of credentials, such as saving passwords in browser stores, poor control mechanisms like permitting autofill for browser-stored credentials, and credential reuse and sharing. Effective secret management begins with a formal process that covers passwords, API keys, tokens, certificates, and other sensitive credentials. By requiring unique passwords for every account and encouraging the use of password managers, organizations reduce the risks associated with credential reuse and sharing. Storing secrets securely, with encryption and access restricted to only those who need them, further strengthens protection. When credentials are no longer necessary or are suspected to be compromised, they should be promptly revoked and replaced. Regular reviews of access to privileged credentials, combined with thorough logging, help detect unusual activity. Equipping staff with training on safe handling and clear reporting procedures ensures that accidental disclosures are addressed quickly and effectively.</p><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/10/2-1-no-copy.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="1875" height="938" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/2-1-no-copy.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/10/2-1-no-copy.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/10/2-1-no-copy.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/10/2-1-no-copy.jpg 1875w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><h2 id="tales-from-the-frontlines-an-exclusive-briefing-on-q3-incidents">Tales from the Frontlines: An exclusive briefing on Q3 incidents</h2><p>On November 12,  Talos incident responders will be hosting an exclusive, unrecorded 30-minute webinar to review the most high-impact incidents our customers faced in Q3. Designed for security professionals of all levels, this is a candid discussion of what happened, how we handled it, and what it means for your organization.</p><p>Registration is required to attend. Don't miss this chance to hear real-world stories from the frontlines of cyber defense, directly from the team who lived them.</p><div class="kg-card kg-button-card kg-align-left"><a href="https://cs.co/IRTales-2026Q3" class="kg-btn kg-btn-accent">Register here</a></div><h2 id="mitre-attck-appendix">MITRE ATT&amp;CK appendix&nbsp;</h2><p>The table below represents MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques and tools observed in this quarter’s IR engagements, including relevant examples. Given that some techniques can fall under multiple tactics, we grouped them under the most relevant tactic based on the way they were leveraged. Please note that this is not an exhaustive list.</p><p>Key findings from the MITRE ATT&amp;CK framework include:</p><ul><li>The rise of phishing and the frequent use of valid accounts for initial access and lateral movement both highlight how attackers continue to rely on methods that have remained consistently effective year over year.</li><li>Attacker success consistently reflects basic weaknesses in enterprise security posture, with persistent exploitation of known vulnerabilities — especially in internet-facing applications — demonstrating that adversaries continually scan for and opportunistically target unpatched systems.</li><li>The most used techniques to maintain persistence were deploying web shells and creating scheduled tasks, demonstrating that attackers prioritize stealthy, reliable access by leveraging legitimate system components that blend in with normal operations. These techniques enable threat actors to maintain long-term control and quickly re-establish their presence even if some signs of compromise are removed.</li></ul>

    <script>
  function coverageView() {
    const coverageTable = document.getElementById('coverage-block');
    const sidebar = document.getElementById('side-bar');
    const postContent = document.getElementById('post-content');
    const currentWidth = window.innerWidth;  
  
    if (coverageTable && sidebar && postContent) {
      if (currentWidth < 1000) {
        // For viewport less than 1000px, append to post content
        postContent.appendChild(coverageTable);
      } else {
        // For viewport 1000px and wider, check for subscribe button
        const subscribeButton = sidebar.querySelector('.subscribe-button');
        
        if (subscribeButton) {
          // Insert coverage block after the subscribe button
          subscribeButton.insertAdjacentElement('afterend', coverageTable);
        } else {
          // No subscribe button found, prepend to top of sidebar
          sidebar.prepend(coverageTable);
        }
      }
    }
  }
  
  window.addEventListener("resize", coverageView);
  window.addEventListener("load", coverageView);


  (function() {
    Array.from(document.querySelectorAll("a")).filter(a => {
        return a.hostname && a.hostname !== location.hostname
    }).forEach(a => a.setAttribute('target', '_blank'))
})();
</script>
